Four attempts at one review frame, and the model degraded monotonically rather than converging: attempt 1 returned a **complete, valid verdict** (7,815 chars, both slots, every required field) which the runtime discarded on the `state-digest` concurrency check; attempt 2 came back truncated mid-string (5,214 chars, unparseable); attempt 3 was 465 chars with the verdict object present but `escalation`, `escalation-reason` and the blocker's `root-cause` dropped; attempt 4 was 42 chars — `{"review-verdict":"revise","leftovers":[]}` — the verdict collapsed from an object to a scalar. Retries exhausted, run recorded `rejected` under a schema error that is the last symptom of the collapse, not its cause.

**THE DELIVERY GAP — measured, and harness-dependent.** `prepare_correction_retry` (modules/cli/src/app/drive.rs:3553) has two modes. Resumed (a `session-flag`/`resume-flag` plus an observed session id) replaces the ENTIRE prompt with the correction string — no frame prompt, no contract section, no resolved inputs. Cold start (either missing) rebuilds the full frame prompt with the correction appended. It passes `schema: Some(ctx.schema)` either way, but `harness_argv` only emits it `if let (Some(flag), Some(schema))` (drive.rs:3454) — and **`json_schema_flag` is `None` for opencode (harness_config.rs:4392) and pi (:4417), set only for claude-code (:4364)**. So on the two `--session` harnesses the schema is silently dropped and a resumed retry delivers exactly one sentence. Run-7619031a's attempt 4 argv is the proof: `opencode run --format json --thinking --auto --dir … --session ses_… "<correction>"`, with `input: 1,192` tokens against `cache read: 14,848`. The entire shape contract lived only in session history — which by then was ~370k tokens deep (that session grew 319,793 → 321,517 → 348,091 → 349,539 → 370,478 across the run's review frames).

**THE INVERSION.** The mode that carries the full contract — cold start — is the DEGRADED fallback, reached only when resuming is impossible (drive.rs:3588-3591: *"A resumed conversation already received the role's standing instructions on its first turn"*). That assumption is sound for cost and false in exactly the two conditions this incident hit at once: a harness that drops the schema, and a conversation long enough that the original contract no longer steers output. P464 chose "a correction is a quick reshape, never a cold full redo" — correct as a default, wrong as the only option.

**Recorded, deliberately NOT in this group** (same incident pair, separate mechanisms, no phase yet): (a) the **prompt-limit band** — `COMMAND_CAPTURE_LIMIT = 262_144` (modules/io/src/run.rs:30) is exactly 2× `DEFAULT_MAX_INLINE_PROMPT_BYTES = 131_072` (frame_prompt.rs:15), so any captured value between them assembles into a prompt that can never be dispatched; run-73b73cc4 died there with a 213,976-byte `slot:review-diff` in a 235,698-byte body, and `resolve_input_value_tokens` (frame_prompt.rs:718-752) applies no per-value cap on that path. (b) the **persistent-session ceiling** — `session-mode=persistent` seats accumulate context across every frame with no reset, no budget, and no honest park; they fail as garbled model output instead of a typed "context exhausted".

- [x] **P540** — The correction carries the contract, on every harness.
      **Why:** a retry that removes the schema and the frame contract is asking a model to fix a shape it can no longer see. On opencode and pi that is what every resumed correction does today, and the failure is invisible because `harness_argv` drops the schema silently rather than reporting that it cannot deliver it.
      **Where:** the shape contract must survive every retry regardless of harness capability. Where `json_schema_flag` exists (claude-code), keep emitting it. Where it does NOT (opencode, pi), the schema is composed INTO the correction body — the same requested-output schema `frame_contract_section` already builds, so no second source of truth. Then make contract-carrying escalation a first-class step rather than an accident of capability: the FIRST correction may stay a bare reshape (P464's cheap path, which is right when the conversation is healthy), and any SUBSEQUENT attempt re-asserts the full frame contract — inputs, requested outputs, schema — whether or not the harness session resumed. A silent capability gap becomes a reported one: when the schema cannot be delivered by flag and is not inlined, that is a `CapabilityReport`, not a shrug.
      **Watch:** do not simply always cold-start — that throws away P464's whole point and re-pays the frame prompt on every trivial reshape; the escalation ladder is the design, and its rungs must be visible in the drive report so a run's transcript shows which retry got what. Inlining a schema into a saturated session makes the context problem WORSE, which is the honest argument for the ceiling work recorded in the group preamble — this phase must not paper over it. Measure the token cost of the escalated retry against a bare one before defaulting the ladder on.
      **Done when:** a resumed correction retry on opencode carries the requested-output schema; a second correction on any harness carries the full frame contract; a harness that can deliver neither reports it; the drive report names, per retry, which rung it used.
- [x] **P541** — Corrections state the defect and the fix, not the validator's internal phrasing.
      **Why:** three of the four correction classes describe the rejection instead of restating what a correct answer looks like, and one of them forwards a core-internal string verbatim. Run-7619031a's attempt 2 was told `call state-digest does not match loaded run session` — a message written for a log reader, naming no slot, no field, and no action. The `MissingSlot` text (drive.rs:2151-2156) names top-level property names and then says *"no other fields, no prose, no code fences"*: when the failure is that the model has LOST the schema, that is a minimization instruction, and attempt 4's scalar verdict is the literal, obedient result of following it.
      **Where:** each `RejectionClass` (drive.rs:3468-3506) gets a correction written for the model that must act on it — naming the ref, the field, what was received and what shape is required. `ContentRejection` currently forwards core's reason (e.g. "inline-field schema expects a JSON object", modules/core/src/procedure/runtime/schema_validation.rs:636): core's `SchemaValidation` already carries `ref_text` and `schema_ref` alongside that reason, so the correction can name the slot and the expected shape without inventing a second diagnosis — the rule is that a model-facing correction is DERIVED from typed rejection evidence, never a raw internal string. Drop the "no other fields" phrasing where it fights the actual defect.
      **Watch:** ALSO TRACE, from this incident, why the `{received}` clause (drive.rs:2138-2150, `parsed.observed_keys`) was EMPTY on attempts 3 and 4 — attempt 3's output parsed with both `review-verdict` and `leftovers` present, so the model was told nothing about what it had actually sent, at exactly the moment that information mattered most. Either `observed_keys` is not populated on that path or the class is misassigned; both are cheap to establish and either changes the message. Corrections stay short — this is not a licence to re-paste the whole contract into every message (that is P540's escalation ladder, deliberately separate).
      **Done when:** no model-facing correction is a verbatim internal runtime string; a content rejection names the ref, the offending field, and the required shape; the `observed_keys` gap is explained and fixed or documented as correct.
- [x] **P542** — A rejection the model cannot act on never spends a model retry.
      **Why:** `StaleIdentity` tells the model *"resubmit against the refreshed frame"* — and never gives it the refreshed frame. Nothing the model can emit satisfies it, so the retry is guaranteed waste, and in run-7619031a it was worse than waste: it discarded a complete, valid verdict (attempt 1, 7,815 chars, every required field) and spent the only high-quality answer the seat produced while it still had context headroom. Every later attempt fought a saturated session. `OutputTruncated` has the same defect in a weaker form: re-asking in the identical session with the identical context pressure changes nothing that caused the truncation.
      **Where:** split the rejection classes by WHO can fix them. Model-fixable (`MissingSlot`, `UnparseableOutput`, `ContentRejection`) keep the correction-retry budget. Runtime-caused (`StaleIdentity`) does NOT consume `max_retries`: it re-dispatches the refreshed frame — the actual remedy — or fails with a typed runtime reason, and never presents itself to the model as the model's mistake. `OutputTruncated` must change a condition before retrying (fresh conversation, reduced attached evidence) or escalate rather than re-ask identically. The distinction becomes visible in the drive report so a transcript never again shows a run "retried 3 times" when zero of those retries could have succeeded.
      **Watch:** this is adjacent to but NOT the same as fixing the `state-digest` guard itself (why a valid submission raced at all is its own question — `ledger_digest` covers the whole serialized `State`, so any concurrent mutation invalidates an in-flight frame); this phase only stops a runtime race from being charged to the model. Preserve the honest-failure rule: a run that cannot proceed parks with a typed reason, never silently continues on a rejected frame.
      **Done when:** a `StaleIdentity` rejection re-dispatches or parks without decrementing the model retry budget; `OutputTruncated` never re-asks under identical conditions; the drive report distinguishes model-fixable from runtime-caused rejections, and a replay of run-7619031a's shape spends its retries only on attempts a model could have satisfied.

## Group 133: The run tells its whole story — merge joins the journey, and the story is a surface you land on (owner-ordered 2026-07-27)

Measured 2026-07-27. **The merge is already typed but invisible while it happens.** `MergeFrame` (stage, status, reason, evidence, `park_reason`, per-hunk decision log) is a first-class part of the session model (`modules/core/src/procedure/session.rs:557+`), and `ctx traits story` already renders merge frames after the fact (`story.rs:14,151-159` via `merge_story`). What does not exist is any presence DURING the merge: `merge.rs` emits **one dim stderr progress line every ~60 seconds** of a merger call (`:3028`) and nothing else — no activity events, no narration, no live-view rows. So a run's visible journey ends at the commit, and then the terminal goes quiet for however long landing takes.

**The storytelling half is landed at its bottom rung and missing its top.** `ctx traits story <run-id>` shipped in P383 (`09a9d3a`) with `--json`/`--markdown`; P521 charters the same command at three depths (default / detailed / **assisted**, the narrator-written prose), and is still open. This group does NOT re-charter that — it consumes it, and adds the two things P521 deliberately leaves out: WHERE the story appears without being asked for, and HOW you reach it from the dashboard.

**Substrate that makes this possible now:** P504's normalized activity model landed (`1780206` — `modules/core/src/procedure/activity.rs`, `modules/io/src/harness_activity.rs`), which is the one vocabulary the narrator, the live view and the story all read. Merge is the last major operation that does not speak it.

- [ ] **P549** — The merge is part of the run, not what happens after it.
      **Why:** landing is where a run most often goes wrong — gate failures, reconciliation conflicts, standing-wall refusals, cleanup parks — and it is the least observable thing the product does. The evidence already exists as typed `MergeFrame`s; what is missing is that the merge never joins the activity stream, so the narrator has nothing to narrate, the live view has no rows to draw, and the owner watches a silent terminal with a dim line once a minute. A merge that parks after eight minutes should read as a step of the journey that stopped, not as an absence of output.
      **Where:** merge emits P504 activity events at its own stage boundaries — preflight, reconciliation (including each `--deep` merger call), gate execution with the gate's own command evidence, landing, cleanup — mapping `MergeStage`/`MergeStatus` onto the same vocabulary a driven frame uses, so every existing consumer inherits it with no per-surface special-casing. The live view gains merge rows in the same pane as the run's steps (the journey is one sequence, not two), the narrator narrates them like any other activity, and the ~60s dim stderr line is DELETED rather than kept alongside. `merge_story`'s plain-language sentences (P472) are the wording source — a stage that parks says the same sentence live as it does in `story` and on the MERGES screen.
      **Watch:** the merger agent must keep dispatching through its normal tool-capable convention, never the narrator's tool-less one (`merge.rs:2776-2778`) — narrating a merge must not change how the merge runs. `--json` and non-TTY output stay byte-stable; this adds a live channel, it does not restyle the report. The merge holds a lock and can run concurrently with other runs (`merge_lock.rs`), so activity must be attributed to the run being landed, never to whichever process happens to be watching.
      **Done when:** a `--worktree --merge` run shows landing as continuous rows in the same live view as its steps, narrated; a parked merge names its stage and reason live in the same words `story` uses; the dim 60-second line is gone; `--json` output is unchanged.
- [ ] **P550** — The story is a place you land, not a command you remember.
      **Why:** the run ends, the pane closes, and everything the owner watched is gone — the story exists but only if you know the command and the run id. Two asks, one shape: at the end of a run (default-on, or `--story`) the narrator's assisted story should open in a scrollable window, and from the dashboard a single key on a run should do the same against its receipt.
      **Where:** (a) a `--story` flag on `run`, plus a config default so it is on without asking, rendering P521's **assisted** level into a scrollable pane at run end — after the merge when one runs, so the story covers the landing P549 just made narratable, not just the work. (b) a dashboard key on a session row that renders the same story for that receipt. **The key is `S` (shift-s), owner-decided 2026-07-27** — lowercase `s` is already `open_resume_modal` on the Sessions screen (`dashboard.rs:1838`) and stays as it is; `1 2 3 4 a A b c d e j k m n o p q r s v x y z` are all bound, so `S` is the free neighbour of the verb it reads like. Do not overload `s` with a modal-vs-story branch.
      **Watch:** `assisted` is the only level that spends tokens (P521's own rule) — a default-on story must therefore either default to the FREE level and offer assisted on request, or the config default must be explicit that it costs model calls; decide that in-phase and state it, because "storytelling is on by default" and "every run silently makes an extra model call" are the same sentence read two ways. The story is read-only and never advances a session.
      **Where — EVERY terminal state tells its story (owner-decided 2026-07-27), and this is a requirement, not a nicety.** Parked, blocked, exhausted, harness-failed and cancelled runs all render, and they are the cases the story exists for: a run that ends unapproved is exactly when the owner needs to read what happened without reconstructing it from a ledger. Wiring the story to the success path would invert its value — the shape to avoid is "story on completion", the shape to build is "story on termination, whatever the disposition". The story must state that disposition plainly in its own opening rather than reading like a success narrative with a bad ending.
      **Done when:** a run with `--story` (or the config default) ends by opening a scrollable, human-readable story that includes the merge; `S` on a dashboard session row renders the same for any past run from its receipt; the token cost of the assisted level is explicit wherever it is enabled by default; and a parked, failed or cancelled run tells its story with its disposition named up front.

## Group 134: Packages worth shipping — a Rust diagnostics loop, a task-authoring trait, and the toolkit the others are cut from (owner-ordered 2026-07-28)

Three packages, one theme: what a user installs on day one. `@ctx-traits/rust` exists today as **two role factories and nothing else** (`packages/rust/src/index.ts`, 0.1.0-alpha.0) despite a README promising "a complete vendorable canonical trait package" — the gap between that claim and that file is a release problem on its own. `@ctx-traits/agents` holds the real reusable material (role factories, doctrine strings, blocker/leftover/verdict schemas, `guardedProduction`, `commitTail`) under a name that describes none of it. And there is no way to write down work for a later run except by hand-editing a gitignored plan.

**Authoring doctrine is binding on all three** (P533, `.docs/PRODUCT.md`): `index.ts` declares only; nouns in `data.ts`; shapes in `schema.ts`; roles in `agent.ts`; one `sequence/<concept>.ts` per step named for the domain concept. Split by entity/domain/intent, share by primitive, never oversplit — a module earns its filename with a domain word, and one imported once with no domain name gets inlined. Fewer, well-named files beat many tidy ones.

- [ ] **P553** — `@ctx-traits/rust`: the compiler is the reviewer.
      **Why:** a Rust fix loop whose blockers come from `cargo` rather than from a model is the strongest demo the product has — every finding carries a file, a line, a column and a compiler-assigned code, so "did the run fix it?" is answered by re-running the compiler instead of asking an agent. It is also the honest completion of a package whose README already claims it.
      **Where — THE SHAPE, and the parsing constraint is the whole design.** `cargo check --message-format json` and `cargo clippy --message-format json` emit **NDJSON — one JSON object per line, 226 lines on this repo today, the large majority of them `compiler-artifact` and `build-script-executed` noise rather than diagnostics.** A typed command output slot receives PARSED stdout and expects exactly one JSON value, so raw cargo output cannot land in one: it must arrive in a `schema:text` slot (the raw-stdout route) and be reduced by a deterministic step, never by a model. The reduction: keep only `reason == "compiler-message"`, project each to `{file, line, column, code, level, message}` from `message.spans[0]` plus `message.code.code`, drop anything without a primary span, and **deduplicate** — the same diagnostic is emitted once per compilation unit that sees it, and a macro-expanded lint repeats per expansion site. Key on `(file, line, column, code)`. Sort deterministically so two runs over the same tree produce byte-identical evidence.
      **Where — FORMAT SETTLED (measured 2026-07-28, do not re-litigate).** Use plain `--message-format json`. Cargo offers `human`, `short`, `json`, `json-diagnostic-short`, `json-diagnostic-rendered-ansi`, `json-render-diagnostics`; only the `json*` family is machine-readable and only `json-diagnostic-short` is a plausible alternative. It is 27% smaller (2,886 → 2,102 bytes on a two-diagnostic probe) but the saving comes entirely from shortening `rendered`, which is just 12% of a diagnostic. The actual bulk is `code.explanation` (33% — the full prose essay for the error code, repeated per diagnostic) and `spans` (35%), and `json-diagnostic-short` shortens neither. Since the reduce step discards `rendered`, `explanation`, and every span past the primary anyway, the projected form is ~90 bytes per diagnostic — **8% of raw** — so pre-shrinking a field that is about to be dropped buys nothing. Two further reasons plain `json` is correct rather than merely adequate: `json-diagnostic-short` is a RENDERING variant, so depending on it couples the parser to presentation formatting instead of structure; and keeping `rendered` leaves a ready-made human-readable block if a later surface wants to show what was fixed. The real capture-size lever is not the format flag at all — 198 of 226 lines on this repo are `compiler-artifact`/`build-script-executed` noise, removed by `--quiet` and by dropping every non-`compiler-message` line at the reduce step.
      **Where — the loop.** `capture (check + clippy) → reduce → for-each over diagnostics or one batched fix frame → re-run both → exit when the reduced list is empty`, bounded, with `onExhausted: "block"` — a run that ends with diagnostics outstanding has not fixed them and must not claim it did. Both a standalone trait (`rust-fix` or similar) and an exported sequence factory the toolkit can compose, per the owner's "probably both": the factory takes the cargo invocation and the round budget, the trait is the thin package that wires it to a worker seat.
      **Watch:** `--message-format json` is a stable contract but the diagnostic SHAPE is not fully stable across toolchains — pin what is read (`reason`, `message.code.code`, `message.level`, `message.spans[].{file_name,line_start,column_start}`) and degrade loudly on an unrecognized shape rather than silently dropping findings; a fix loop that quietly ignores diagnostics is worse than no loop. `cargo` exits non-zero when diagnostics exist, so the capture step's success criterion must NOT be exit code 0 or the first frame never runs — this is what `sequence.check`'s exit-code verdict is for, separately from the capture itself. Clippy and check overlap: dedup across both, do not run two loops. `--fix` exists and is tempting; the phase's value is the agent explaining and fixing, so if `--fix` is used at all it is as a pre-pass whose result is re-captured, never as the fix step. Warnings versus errors is a policy call the phase must state: default to errors-block, warnings-report, and let the caller widen it.
      **Done when:** one command reduces a real `cargo check`+`clippy` run on this repo into a deduplicated, deterministically ordered diagnostic list; a bounded loop drives an agent through them and exits only when a fresh capture is clean; the same machinery is reachable as both a trait and an exported factory; and a run that cannot clear every diagnostic blocks rather than committing.
- [ ] **P554** — The task-authoring trait: work written down where a later run can find it.
      **Why:** there is no supported way to record work for a future run. The execution plan lives under `/.plans/`, which is gitignored, so a `--worktree` run's edit to it is discarded with the worktree — P518 burned 116k tokens discovering exactly that, and the checkbox has never once been written for the same reason. A `tasks/` directory at the repo root is TRACKED, so a task file survives the merge, arrives in the diff, and is readable by the next run and by a human in a PR.
      **Where:** a trait that takes a request, investigates the repository with its own tools, and writes `tasks/NNNN-<slug>.md` — zero-padded sequence discovered by scanning the directory (never assumed, never passed in), slug derived from the title. The body is written FOR `implement`, not for a human skim: scope, the files it will touch with paths, the approach, what proves it (the exact gate commands), risks, and an explicit out-of-scope section. The point is that a later `implement` run needs no further clarification — if the task file leaves the implementer guessing, the trait has failed even though it produced a file. Emits the path as a typed output port so a caller can chain it.
      **Watch:** the numbering has a real race — two concurrent runs both scan, both see `0007`, both write `0008-…`. Deriving the number inside a `command` step from the directory listing at write time narrows it; state the residual risk rather than pretending it is gone, and never silently overwrite an existing file. Do NOT let this trait implement anything: its deliverable is the description, and a run that starts editing source has misunderstood its own contract. Do NOT reinvent the plan's phase grammar — a task file is a request for work, not a phase contract with Done-when/Deps, and the two must not drift into the same thing. `tasks/` must be committed (it is not currently in `.gitignore`; verify before the first run).
      **Done when:** the trait produces a numbered, slugged, tracked task file whose content is sufficient for an `implement` run to work from with no further input; concurrent runs never collide silently; and the file lands through a normal worktree merge.
- [ ] **P555** — `@ctx-traits/toolkit`: the package the others are cut from.
      **Why:** the reusable material exists and is misfiled. `@ctx-traits/agents` holds role factories, eight doctrine strings, five schemas, `guardedProduction` and `commitTail` under a name that describes only one of those things — and every family trait imports from it, so its name is now the first thing an external author reads. The toolkit is that material, named for what it is, plus the patterns the store has proven and never packaged.
      **Where — what goes in, cherry-picked from what already works.** ROLES: the `workerRole`/`reviewerRole`/`scribeRole`/`clerkRole` factories (agents) and the Rust-specialized pair (rust). SCHEMAS: `blockerSchema`, `reviewVerdictSchema`, `leftoverSchema`, `ownerItemSchema`, plus the diagnostic shape P553 defines. SEQUENCE FACTORIES: `guardedProduction` and `commitTail` (agents/process.ts), the gate-and-diff evidence pair every implement variant repeats, the deterministic park-report projection, and P553's capture-reduce-fix loop. PARALLEL PATTERNS: the store uses `sequence.parallel` and `forEach` nowhere despite both being landed CDK primitives — fan-out-then-join over a typed list, and per-item processing with a joined result, are exactly the shapes a toolkit should make one call.
      **Where — the migration, and it is the risky half.** Every `.ctx/traits/*` package imports from `@ctx-traits/agents` today; moving that surface moves every consumer's imports and therefore every canonical digest, which means a rebuild and a re-approval of the whole store. Decide in-phase between: (a) rename `agents` → `toolkit` and re-export from the old name for one release, or (b) leave `agents` in place and build `toolkit` as the new home that re-exports from it during transition. (b) is lower risk and probably right for a release; (a) is cleaner and can follow. Whichever is chosen, ONE package is the source of truth for each symbol — a re-export is a pointer, never a second copy.
      **Watch:** a toolkit is where over-abstraction goes to hide. Only material with TWO existing consumers earns a place; anything with one gets inlined at its single call site, per the pragmatism rule. Doctrine strings are the sharpest case: `implement-quick` was cut from five spliced blocks to zero on 2026-07-27 and reads better for it, so the toolkit exports them as available material and must not imply that composing them is good practice. This lands ahead of the family fold (P530/P531) or after it, not during — the fold rewrites the same imports, and doing both at once makes every failure ambiguous.
      **Done when:** one package holds the reusable roles, schemas, sequence factories and parallel patterns, each with exactly one definition; the family traits build against it unchanged in behavior; the parallel patterns have at least one real consumer rather than shipping unexercised; and an external author reading the package name can tell what is inside it.

## Group 135: Running out is a first-class outcome — subscription pressure and per-account routing (owner-ordered 2026-07-28)

Two halves of the same problem: a run today discovers it has no capacity by failing, and an owner with several accounts has no way to spread load across them. The evidence for the first half already exists and is thrown away — `claude-code`'s stream emits `rate_limit_event` with a full usage picture on nearly every turn, and **`rg rate_limit modules/` returns nothing**: ctx never reads it. Sampled from this repo's own debug traces: `{"status":"allowed_warning","resetsAt":1785452400,"rateLimitType":"seven_day","utilization":0.98,"isUsingOverage":false,"surpassedThreshold":0.75}` — 960 `allowed_warning`, 684 `allowed`, and **41 `rejected`** events across four limit types (`five_hour`, `seven_day`, `seven_day_overage_included`), with utilization observed up to 0.99.

- [ ] **P556** — Subscription pressure is a run signal, not a surprise.
      **Why:** `provider_error.rs` classifies `CreditsExhausted` only for API-key providers, by string-matching a refusal AFTER it happens — `"requires more credits"`, `"insufficient_quota"`, `"credit balance is too low"` — and every one of those is a post-mortem: the turn is already lost. Subscription harnesses (claude-code, and the same question for codex) fail differently: they degrade toward a hard `rejected` that the drive loop currently reads as an ordinary `harness-failed`, spending retries on a condition no retry can clear. Meanwhile the stream has been telling us the exact utilization, the limit type and the reset epoch for the entire run, and we discard it. An owner watching a 1h20m run deserves to know at 0.98 that the next frame will not start.
      **Where:** decode `rate_limit_event` into the P504 activity model so utilization, limit type and `resetsAt` are first-class run evidence — visible live, persisted in the ledger, and available to `story`. Then act on it at the frame boundary, which is the only safe place: **`rejected` → pause the run, do not retry** (typed and resumable, with `resetsAt` recorded so the run states when it can continue, rather than dying as `harness-failed`); **`allowed_warning` above a configurable threshold → surface it and let the owner decide**, since the natural default of "pause at 0.95" would have paused runs that then completed fine. Extend `CreditsExhaustedEvidence` (or add a sibling) so subscription exhaustion carries `resets-at` rather than a top-up URL — telling a subscription owner to top up is wrong advice.
      **Watch:** never pause MID-frame — a frame is an atomic harness subprocess and interrupting it loses the work; the check belongs where the next frame would be dispatched. `utilization` is per-limit-type and four types were observed, including an overage variant, so "the" utilization is a false singular — read them separately and pause on whichever actually rejects. `resetsAt` is an epoch and must never be converted to a duration in the ledger (P510's clock-in-evidence rule). Do NOT auto-resume when the reset passes in this phase: a run that resumes unattended at 3am against a fresh weekly window is a surprise of a different kind — record readiness, let the owner or a later phase resume. Codex and opencode emit different (or no) usage telemetry; this phase does claude-code properly and reports an explicit `unsupported` capability for the rest rather than pretending parity.
      **Done when:** a run's ledger and live view carry utilization/limit-type/reset evidence from the harness stream; a `rejected` rate-limit pauses the run with a typed reason naming when it can resume, consuming no retries; the warning threshold is configurable and off by default; and a harness without usage telemetry says so as a capability rather than silently behaving as if it has headroom.
- [ ] **P557** — One harness, several accounts: per-seat credential routing.
      **Why:** an owner with more than one subscription cannot use the second one. A harness is resolved by binary name and inherits whatever ambient credentials that binary finds — so every seat in every run shares one account, and the only way to switch is to log out and back in, which (observed 2026-07-28) kills every in-flight run's next worker frame with `Failed to authenticate`. Two accounts should mean two seats that can run concurrently, and P556's pressure signal is what makes routing between them worth having.
      **Where:** a per-seat credential/proxy binding in `[agent.role.*]` alongside `harness`/`model` — the seat names WHICH credential context its spawns use, and the spawner applies it per process rather than relying on ambient login state. The mechanism is per-harness and must be established from each harness's real surface, not assumed: an env-var credential path, a config-home override, or an HTTP proxy in front of the binary are all plausible and only some will exist for each of claude-code/codex/opencode/pi. Establish what each actually supports FIRST and report `unsupported` for the ones that support none — a routing layer that silently falls back to ambient credentials is worse than no routing, because it looks like isolation and is not.
      **Watch:** credentials must never reach the ledger, the debug traces, or a prompt — the spawn already records argv verbatim into `~/.config/ctx/debug/**`, so a token passed as an argument would be written to disk in cleartext; env or file-path indirection only, and the redaction path needs testing rather than assuming. This is adjacent to but NOT the same as the P480 sandbox: confinement controls what a spawn may WRITE, this controls what it authenticates AS. Concurrency is the real prize and the real risk — two seats on two accounts running at once is the point, but they share one `merge_lock` and one run store, so prove the isolation is per-spawn and not per-process before promising it. Rotation-on-exhaustion (P556 pauses, this could instead re-route) is a tempting follow-up and explicitly NOT in this phase: get deterministic, declared routing working before anything automatic decides which account to burn next.
      **Done when:** two seats in one config can bind different credential contexts for the same harness binary; a run using them authenticates independently per spawn; no credential material appears in argv, ledger or debug traces; and each harness reports honestly whether it supports the mechanism at all.

## Group 136: A reviewer that cannot see its own last verdict — optional refs, and the blocker that vanished (diagnosed 2026-07-28 from run-3d5ef0a1ff)

**The incident, traced end to end.** P530's fourth attempt ran four rounds. Round 2's reviewer raised `implement-fold-not-landed` — a perfect blocker: *"The phase's deliverable does not exist: there is no `.ctx/traits/implement` family package … none of P530's done-when criteria are met."* Round 3's reviewer raised two unrelated Stage-B defects and **did not re-raise it**. Round 4 approved with zero blockers, demoting the missing fold to advisory prose about what to do *"before Stage C"*. The commit landed, the run reported `completed`, and `.ctx/traits/implement` still does not exist.

**Why, mechanically — and it is not a judgment failure.** The built canonical wires the review step as `input = [port:phase, slot:draft, slot:work-summary, slot:review-diff, slot:repo-gates-passed, resource:product-doc]`, `output = [slot:review-verdict]`. **The reviewer never reads `slot:review-verdict`.** Every write is `op=replace` (confirmed in that run's `slot-revisions`: four rounds, `replace` every time), so round 3's reviewer opened with no record that a blocker had ever been raised, re-derived a verdict from the current diff, and overwrote the old one. The blocker was not dropped — it was invisible. This also explains why `recurrence-of` never fires reliably: that field asks the reviewer to compare against the previous round's blockers, which it cannot see.

**Why the obvious fix does not compile, and what that revealed.** Interpolating the verdict into the review prompt yields `step 'building-review' reads slot:review-verdict, first produced by later step 'building-review'`. But that check (`modules/core/src/trait/procedure/validate.rs:3046-3050`) filters to **required** inputs only — `.filter(|input| !input.is_optional())` — and a hand-patched canonical carrying `{ slot = "slot:review-verdict", optional = true }` on the review step **passes `ctx traits check` clean**. The pattern is already in production: `building-produce` reads that same slot optionally, produced by a *later* step in the same body. So the model has always allowed loop-carried self-reads; only the AUTHORING surface cannot express one, because every `${ref}` in a template mints a required input.

- [ ] **P558** — `slot.optional()`: optionality is a per-site wrapper, usable where refs are actually written.
      **Why:** the concept exists (`input.optional(slot)` → `{ slot, optional: true }`, per-site, so one slot is required in one step and optional in another) but is reachable only from a step's `input:` array. Refs are written inside prompt text, so the ergonomic gap is what forced the reviewer's blind spot: an author who wants a loop-carried read must drop out of the template and hand-maintain a parallel `input:` array, and nobody did.
      **Where:** add `.optional()` to the slot handle returning the existing `OptionalSlotInputValue` shape, and teach `prompt.template`/`prompt.text` ref collection (`packages/cdk/src/prompt.ts:327,335`, via `collectMany`) to emit the optional form into the step's `input` array instead of a required entry. Canonical output is unchanged from what the validator already accepts — `{ slot = "…", optional = true }` — so this is an authoring-surface addition, not a model change.
      **Watch:** the one real design decision is what an absent optional ref RENDERS as on round 1. The `input:`-array form sidesteps it by attaching values as frame context rather than splicing into prose; the template form must pick and state a rule (render as nothing, or a named placeholder) and apply it everywhere. Do NOT let `.optional()` become a way to silence the producer-before-consumer check on genuinely mis-ordered steps — it is exempt because "read the current value if one exists" is meaningful, and that meaning only holds inside a loop or after a pre-seed; flag an optional self-read outside both as the authoring error it is.
- [ ] **P559** — The reviewer reads its own last verdict, and one line does it.
      **Why:** with P558, carrying an unresolved blocker forward needs no ledger, no accumulator and no doctrine — the reviewer simply sees what it said last round and restates what it cannot verify as fixed. Replace semantics stop mattering the moment the value is read back.
      **Where:** the review prompt gains the ref and nothing else: `` `… Your previous verdict: ${verdict.optional()}` ``. Apply to `implement-quick` and the shared family review builder (`implement-default/source/shared.ts`'s `reviewSeat`). **No accompanying prose paragraph** — a reviewer that can see it said "the fold is missing" does not need to be told to care, and explaining at length why prior verdicts matter is the same compensating-for-a-missing-input mistake in a different form.
      **Watch:** rebuild moves every implement digest and needs re-approval. Verify against the real failure: a run whose round-2 blocker goes unfixed must still carry it at round 3 — the recorded shape of run-3d5ef0a1ff is the regression test. This makes `recurrence-of` meaningful for the first time (the reviewer can finally compare), but do NOT re-add the recurrence breaker to `implement-quick` on that basis — it was removed by owner decision 2026-07-27 because it killed a converging loop.

## Group 137: Frame render v2 — the driven frame reads like the trait does (owner-designed 2026-07-28)

The behavioral render became a tagged XML document in P493. The DRIVEN FRAME never did: it is still the debug-shaped block a runtime author would write, and a real worker frame measures **49,153 chars** split as — step/guard header 480, available-inputs list 387, requested outputs 49, prompt instructions 16,965, **resolved input values 26,547**, and **input JSON Schema 4,725**. Roughly 5.7k of that is envelope, and the largest pure waste is the input-schema block: the full nested JSON Schema of every INPUT, including descriptions, for data the model reads and never produces.

**The agreed shape** (owner, 2026-07-28) — XML for structure, JSON for values, one representation throughout:

```
<input>
  <prompt>Use [review-verdict] as the prior review result. …</prompt>
  <data>
    <review-verdict>{ "status": "revise", "blockers": [ … ] }</review-verdict>
  </data>
</input>
<output>
  <prompt>Put your thoughts into [foo] and details into [bar].</prompt>
  <format>{ "foo": string, "bar": bar }</format>
  <schema><bar>{ "baz": string, "qux": number }</bar></schema>
</output>
```

**Four decisions settled in the design conversation, recorded so they are not re-opened.** (1) Data values stay JSON inside their element — never XML-ified into repeated child elements: arrays, empty strings and nulls do not round-trip, and the model must REPLY in JSON, so showing input as XML teaches the wrong shape. (2) `<data>` items carry NO schema attribute — input needs the value, not its type; only `<output>` describes shapes. (3) Prompt references become `[slot-id]` brackets pointing at `<data>` elements, replacing today's `{slot:id}` token substitution. (4) The existing content-derived delimiter is KEPT inside untrusted values — it is `Digest::source(kind\nlabel\nbody)` truncated to 12 hex chars (`frame_prompt.rs:1109-1120`), so forging it requires a preimage of the value's own digest, and it is deterministic, so prompt caching still works. An XML `nonce=` attribute was considered and REJECTED: closing tags carry no attributes, so `</work-summary>` would close the element regardless and the nonce would be decorative.

- [ ] **P560** — Delete the input JSON Schema block from the frame contract.
      **Why:** 4,725 chars per frame — measured on a real worker frame — describing the types of values the model has already been handed and will never emit. The `Your contract for this step:` section lists `Input types (JSON Schema)` with every nested field description inlined; nothing consumes it, and no output depends on it. Independent of the rest of this group and landable on its own.
      **Where:** drop the input-types enumeration from `frame_prompt.rs`'s contract section; keep the output contract exactly as-is for now. If a particular input's description is genuinely load-bearing for a step, it belongs in that step's prompt text, authored — not auto-dumped for every input on every frame.
      **Watch:** verify nothing downstream parses that block (narrator, story, proofs) before deleting; it is model-facing text, so the risk is a test pinning it rather than a consumer needing it.
- [ ] **P561** — The `<input>` envelope: prompt, then data, and nothing else.
      **Why:** the frame's first 900 chars are runtime bookkeeping the model cannot act on — `Step [run 1 / source 0]`, `Loop building iteration 2/10`, `Guard all[...] => false (one or more guards did not match)`, and an `Available inputs:` list of refs with digests that the `Resolved input values:` section then repeats in full. Guard evaluation results are debug output; a model reading "Guard … => false" learns nothing it can use.
      **Where:** `<input>` holds `<prompt>` (the step's authored text, with `[slot-id]` references) and `<data>` (one element per available input, id-named, value inlined as JSON, untrusted bodies wrapped in the existing content-derived delimiter). Everything else goes: the guard lines, the duplicated ref list, the digests (the ledger holds provenance — the model does not need it). Absent optional inputs simply have no element, which is what makes `slot.optional()` usable inside prompt text at last: a `[review-verdict]` reference to a missing element is a reference to something not present, rather than the unresolved `{slot:…}` token core currently refuses.
      **Watch:** the P493 escape (`emit.rs::body_integrity` + the `ForgedRenderTag` finding) must apply to the frame envelope's own tag set — reuse that table, never a second one. Keep the content-derived delimiter for untrusted values regardless; escaping is defense in depth on top of preimage resistance, not a replacement for it. Consider inlining short scalar values without the delimiter (a 6-char `"revise"` inside a 40-char wrapper is noise) and applying it above a size threshold where injection actually lives — `work-summary`, `draft`, `review-diff`.
- [ ] **P562** — The `<output>` envelope: format, then only the schemas it references.
      **Why:** today the output contract is a wall of JSON Schema, and the schema reaches the model by flag only on claude-code (`json_schema_flag`); **opencode and pi declare none**, so on those harnesses a resumed correction retry carries no shape contract at all — the P540 gap that degraded run-7619031a from a complete verdict to a scalar. Putting the shape in the prompt fixes every harness at once, including the resumed-retry path.
      **Where:** `<format>` is the literal key skeleton — `{ "foo": string, "bar": bar }` — naming scalar types inline and named types by name. `<schema>` carries ONLY the named types `<format>` actually references, so an all-scalar step emits one line and nested definitions appear solely when used. Keep the `--json-schema` flag where a harness has one; this is additive, not a replacement.
      **Watch:** design for BOTH directions now — the owner's stated direction is that input and output will both be authored with `prompt.text`, so `<output><prompt>` is a first-class authored field from the start, not a later retrofit. Do not let `<format>` drift into a second schema dialect: it is a shape sketch for the model, and the canonical JSON Schema stays the validation authority — the runtime still validates against the schema, never against the sketch.
- [ ] **P563** — The system prompt carries the agent, the goal, and nothing per-frame.
      **Why:** role identity and standing instructions are stable for a whole run, and today they are partly in the per-frame body (`You are agent:worker. Your only responsibility is this step…`) and partly in `--append-system-prompt`. Anything stable belongs in the system prompt: it is the cache-friendly half, and repeating it per frame pays for it every round.
      **Where:** the seat's role, its description, the standing one-frame-at-a-time discipline, and the trait's behavioral render (the `<intent>`/`<behavior>` material P493 already emits, which driven frames do not carry today — see `.plans/FUTURE.md`'s parked persona note) go to the system prompt; the per-frame body carries only what changes. Harnesses without a system-prompt flag keep today's fallback of prepending it to the body, reported as a capability rather than hidden.
      **Watch:** measure before defaulting the persona on — FUTURE.md parked it precisely because it is a behavioral change to every driven frame in every run, and its cost was never measured. This phase may land the SPLIT (stable vs per-frame) without landing the persona, and that is a legitimate outcome; state which was done.

## Group 138: "Leaf" is an implementation word — say family and variants (owner-ordered 2026-07-28)

`trust approve implement` currently reports:

```
  family: implement
  leaves approved: 5
```

"Leaf" is the folded package's internal tree vocabulary, minted by P530's native-family work. Nobody authoring a trait writes it: the CDK surface is `variants: { quick: … }` and `variant.import()`, the config selector is `implement:quick`, and `.docs/PRODUCT.md` calls the axis `<family>-{quick,default,smart,strict}`. A user reading an approval should see the words they already use, and a count is the least useful thing on that line — five WHICH variants is the question trust answers.

- [ ] **P564** — Approval names the variants it approved, in the vocabulary the author already uses.
      **Why:** the approval panel is the one place a machine-global, security-relevant decision is confirmed to a human, and it currently reports an internal noun plus a number. `leaves approved: 5` cannot be checked against anything; `variants: default, quick, smart, strict, phase` can be read against what the author expects the family to contain, which is exactly the check a trust confirmation exists to enable.
      **Where:** the target shape —
      ```
      family: implement
      variants: default, quick, smart, strict, phase
      ```
      Sorted deterministically (the family table is a `BTreeMap`, so selector order is already stable) and rendered through the P465 panel kit like every other row. The emitting site is `handle_trust_approve`'s family branch (`modules/cli/src/app/lifecycle_reporting.rs`), which already has the resolved leaf set in hand — it currently counts it instead of naming it.
      **Where — the sweep, and it is deliberately bounded.** "Leaf" appears in ~77 string literals across `modules/cli/src/app/` and `modules/io/src/`. This phase changes only what a USER READS: the trust panel, and the two family-resolution errors that surface to a user (`run.rs:1222`, `run.rs:1270` — "declares leaf {variant} at {path}" becomes "declares variant {variant} at {path}"). Rust identifiers, struct fields, `family_manifest.rs`'s `FamilyLeaf`/`leaf_for_variant`, and the `[family.leaf.<selector>]` manifest table stay exactly as they are — renaming a serialized manifest key would move every family package's digest and force a re-approval, which is a large cost for an internal noun nobody reads.
      **Watch:** do NOT rename the `[family.leaf.*]` TOML table — it is on-disk format, and P530's fold has already moved these digests once this week. Check the dashboard's TRUST screen and `story` for the same word before declaring the sweep done; a user-facing surface missed here is worse than not starting, because the vocabulary then differs by screen. If a count is genuinely wanted for a large family, show both — the names are the evidence, a number alone is not.
      **Done when:** `ctx traits trust approve implement` prints `family:` and `variants:` with the selector names; no user-facing surface says "leaf" or "leaves"; the manifest key and internal identifiers are untouched; and no package digest moves as a result of this phase.
